The descent is now bounded at 500 levels, and measured across 90 real repositories hand-written code tops out between 60 and 120, so nothing you write is affected and the declaration itself stays indexed.
- Your `.github/`, `.claude/`, `.cursor/` and `.agents/` directories are indexed at last. The index skipped every directory whose name began with a dot, without exception. Measured across 300 real repositories: 19,188 git-tracked source files, in 228 of them, were invisible to `query`, `map`, `semantic` and `impact` — `.github` in 212 repositories, `.claude-plugin` in 53, `.claude` in 39, `.agents` in 27, plus `.opencode`, `.cursor`, `.codex`, `.kiro` and `.pi`. The dot no longer decides when your repository has a `.gitignore`: that file is what says which paths are generated. Checked on the same corpus: 18,432 files regained, and not one file of noise added. Along the way, credential and user-cache directories (`.ssh`, `.gnupg`, `.aws`, `.kube`, `.npm`, `.cache`, and the like) are now never walked, even when you run tokenade from your home directory.
- `tokenade query`, `map`, `semantic` and `impact` can finally see your `bin/` directory. The index skipped a list of directory names — `bin`, `dist`, `build`, `out`, `target`, `vendor`, `project`, `benchmarks` — at any depth and without checking anything. Measured across 300 real repositories: 5,009 git-tracked source files, in 118 of them, were invisible, including 388 under `bin/` in 58 repositories and a `scripts/build/….mjs` lost because `build` happened to be a middle segment. Those names are now set aside only when your repository says nothing: if it has a `.gitignore`, that file decides what is generated. Checked in the other direction on the same corpus: 4,299 files regained, 2 files of noise added. Tool caches (`node_modules`, `__pycache__`, `Pods`, and so on) are still always set aside.
- A password passed to `sshpass` no longer ends up as the name of a command. When a command runs through a wrapper (`sudo`, `timeout`, `nice`, `sshpass`, `env`, `ionice`, …), tokenade looks for the real program behind it to pick the right compactor. It skipped the wrapper's flags but not their values, so `sshpass -p <password> ssh server uptime` was attributed to the password: it became the routing key, a field in the savings journal, a filename in the statistics directory, and could be quoted back in a message addressed to the agent. The same mistake denied those commands their dedicated compactor (`sudo -u root ls -la` fell back to generic folding). Fixed for every wrapper, in both spellings (`-p secret` and `-psecret`).
- No more advice about commands that do not exist. The message flagging a frequent command with no dedicated compactor was also naming fragments of shell syntax — `done;`, `{`, `clean(){`, `-t`, `$B`, `n=$(grep` — whenever the command was too nested to be split. Measured across 25,515 real commands: 128 of the 219 reported names were not programs at all, accounting for 839 reports. A name must now be capable of being a program's.
- Your `.gitignore` is read the way git reads it: in every directory, with anchors and wildcards. Three defects at once. A nested `.gitignore` did nothing, because only the one at the root was read, while 85 of the 272 repositories checked carry at least one (314 files). An anchored pattern lost its anchor as soon as it carried a wildcard or a trailing slash, so `/_*` and `/locales/` matched at any depth, hiding tracked files under `bin/` and `bin/cli/locales/`. And a wildcard anywhere but at an end matched nothing at all: of 16,711 single-segment rules, 389 across 77 repositories compiled to a rule no name could satisfy — `.env.*.local`, `*secret*`, `*.sw?`, `fix-*.js`, a bare `*` — 66 of which name a credential. Along the way, `*.egg-info/` was also demanding a file by that name. Checked against `git check-ignore` on 120 repositories and 41,971 paths: 78 disagreements on wrongly excluded files, now 0, and 0 in the other direction.
- A `!` line in your `.gitignore` re-includes the file again. Negations were simply skipped, so `!.env.example`, `!.vscode/extensions.json` and `!tests/fixtures/**` stayed invisible to the index even though you had explicitly kept them. The last matching pattern now decides, as it does in git. Measured against `git check-ignore` across 120 real repositories and 47,138 paths, the files tokenade wrongly hid drop from 56 to 5.
- Your `.gitignore` is honoured line by line at last. A rule carrying a slash was reduced to its last segment: `docs/build/` ignored every `build` directory in the repository, `src/**/*.rs` matched nothing, and `newenv/*` vanished outright, so the index walked files you had asked it to ignore. Patterns are now compared segment by segment, with `**` covering zero directories or more and `*` staying inside its segment, using git's anchoring. Measured across 570 real `.gitignore` files: of 1,628 rules carrying a slash, 449 were being lost.
- The `Glob` result filter can no longer be switched off by a neighbouring name. Asking for `.github/workflows/*.yml` turned off the masking of `.git/`, because the pattern happened to contain the characters `.git`; likewise `fuzz_targets` for `target/`, and `distill` for `dist/`. The directory must now be named as a whole segment, `{dist,build}/**` included.
- Comment stripping no longer cuts through the strings it passes over. Three cases were producing wrong code in what you read: a JavaScript or TypeScript template literal containing `//` (`` `see // the docs` ``) was truncated from the two slashes, a single-quoted string containing an escaped quote (`'it\'s // fine'`) came back unterminated, and above all a stylesheet lost the end of every rule containing a URL — `url(http://x)` cut at `url(http:`, closing brace included, CSS having no line comment. Measured across 3,535 real files: files losing a backtick drop from 309 to 193, those losing a brace from 207 to 79. Rust lifetimes (`&'static str`) and `//` comments in SCSS and LESS are unchanged.
- `git status` on a working repository costs half as much. The output repeated the same directory line after line; it is now written once per group, with every filename kept, nothing summarised and nothing counted in place of being named: `M crates/cli/src/agent/: local.rs mod.rs providers.rs`. A modified file and an untracked file in the same directory stay on two separate lines, and a rename keeps both its paths. Measured on a repository with 210 entries: 55% less. On a handful of files the original shape is kept, since grouping would gain nothing.
- A minified file no longer fills your context. Reading a bundle (`dist/index.js`, eight lines one of which is 93,000 characters) returned unreadable slices of minified code, because the "first 150 lines, last 50" fallback hands back the entire file when the file has no lines. You now get what a bundle can say about itself — its size, the modules it imports, the names it exports, how many functions it declares — and the bytes stay reachable with `grep` or `Read`. Measured on real files: 332,000 tokens brought down to 700, including the bundles a build tool leaves on a dozen lines rather than one. An ordinary source file, a JSON file, a minified HTML page and a large asset encoded inside a normal file are all unaffected.
- `pip install` folds back down to 90%. Modern pip carries its progress metrics on the same line, and that line was not recognised as pip noise: on a 300-package install, 300 progress lines came through whole. They are now folded into the summary, while a diff header (`--- a/setup.py`) in the middle of pip output stays visible.
- A build log is no longer summarised as a list of files. Output like `processing /src/mod-12.ts with 7 symbols and 1 warnings` was taken for a directory listing and folded to `processing / (4000 entries)`: four thousand lines reduced to a directory name. A line that MENTIONS a path is no longer confused with a line that IS one, and this output goes back through pattern folding, which keeps the shape of the line and its statistics for the same cost. Real listings (`find`, `ls -R`, `<size> <path>`) are unchanged.
- `tokenade skeleton` now understands 57 more languages. The command relied on a parser available for only 16 extensions; everywhere else — Elixir, Lua, Haskell, Clojure, Dart, Julia, Nim, Zig, OCaml, Erlang, Perl, R, PowerShell, shell — it guessed which lines to keep, while the symbol index already knows 73 extensions and the exact shape of a declaration in each. It now uses that knowledge, and always returns the shorter of the two views, so no language can lose out. Measured across 390 real files: 38% less skeleton, with Elixir going from 53% to 29% of the original file.
- A secret written inside an XML tag is now masked. `<password>…</password>`, `<apiKey>…</apiKey>`, `<client-secret>…</client-secret>` — the shape of a Maven `settings.xml`, a .NET `app.config` or a Spring context — passed through every protection intact, because each rule was looking for an assignment (`key = value`), and XML has none. A generic type (`List<Token> tokens = …`) and a placeholder (`<password>${env.PW}</password>`) are left alone, and the closing tag stays in place.
- An `Authorization` header written between quotes is finally masked on the right side. In the most common JSON and JavaScript form — `Authorization: "Bearer <your token>"` — the word `Bearer` was replaced and the token went through in the clear. The token is now masked, the scheme stays readable, so do the quotes, and an interpolation (`` `Bearer ${apiKey}` ``) is no longer touched at all. A fifth fewer maskings across this family of rules, measured on 2,000 third-party source files.
- URLs and type names are no longer redacted. A line like `curl -X GET 'http://localhost:4000/utils/supported_openai_params'` came back as `http://localhost:<redacted>`: the colon in the PORT read as an assignment, and the endpoint is everything the line says. The same for a slightly long type name (`response_model=LiteLLM_ManagedVectorStoreListResponse`). And quoted values — the majority form in JSON, Python and TypeScript — escaped every protection already in place: `"api_key": "null"` was masked. Measured on 2,000 third-party source files containing no secret: a quarter fewer maskings, and 7 more files completely clean. Real keys, base64 blobs and hexadecimal stay masked, quoted or not.
- Fewer pointless maskings in the code you read. A line like `api_key=self.api_key,` or `password=smtp_password` passes a VARIABLE, not a secret, and was being redacted anyway: the line lost its meaning while protecting nothing. A value shaped like a variable name or a dotted path, with no digits at all, is no longer masked. Prefixed tokens (`sk_live_…`, `hf_…`, `npm_…`), base64 blobs, hexadecimal and single-word passphrases stay masked exactly as before. Measured on 1,993 third-party source files containing no secret: 36% fewer maskings on this pass, and 45 files that no longer carry a single mask.
- Reading an `.xlsx`, a `.docx` or an `.epub` finally counts as a saving. The scoring distinguished agents that display a PDF by themselves, where tokenade adds nothing, but applied that exception to EVERY document format. Nothing displays a spreadsheet or a Word document natively: extraction is precisely what makes them readable. Those reads were recorded as zero and so never appeared in `gain`. PDFs keep their original treatment.
- `tokenade search` finally counts what it saves you. The command credited its results using the model reserved for binary documents, which is worth zero for Claude Code, so no search run from the command line ever appeared in `gain` or on the dashboard, however many results it found. It now uses the same scoring as an automatically intercepted search.
- A search that finds nothing is no longer counted as a saving. When you ask several questions in one call, tokenade credits having paid for one round trip instead of N, but it was also counting the queries that came back empty, which save nothing since you will have to ask again. On this machine, 603 search batches had credited more than a million tokens without a single usable result coming out. Only usable results count now, as was already the case for web pages.
- Placeholders are no longer taken for secrets. `export API_KEY=<your key>`, `-H "X-API-KEY: $API_KEY"`, `export OPENAI_API_KEY={master_key}`: a variable reference or a fill-me-in marker, which is what documentation and the shell snippets you read all day are made of. They came back masked; they now come back as written. With the two previous fixes, masking on secret-free code drops from 805 to 597 across a sample of 1,993 real files, a quarter fewer, without a single real credential stopping being masked.
- Masking no longer eats your function calls or your configuration flags. Entropy detection claimed any token of at least 32 characters placed after a `:` or an `=`: a call with a long name came back as `unified_batch_id=<redacted>(job.…)`, and a configuration key named after itself (`"token-efficient-tools-2025-02-19": "token-efficient-tools-2025-02-19"`) lost its value. Measured on 1,993 real source files: a sixth of those maskings disappear. Genuine high-entropy blobs stay masked.
- Secret masking no longer erases pieces of your code. It rests on patterns like `api_key = …`, and that looseness was eating ordinary code: `api_key: Optional[str] = None` came back as `api_key: <redacted> = None`, `password: str | None` lost its type, `OpenAI(api_key=api_key, base_url=…)` lost its argument, and `if api_key == "x":` came out as `if api_key =<redacted> "x":` — not merely masked but syntactically wrong, on code an agent then goes on to edit. Measured on 1,993 real source files: a third of those maskings disappear. Real credentials — prefixed keys, tokens, entropy-bearing passwords — stay masked as before.
- What you record with `tokenade mistake` finally reaches the agent. The command exists so that an abandoned direction is not proposed again in the next session, but nothing read it back: you had to type `tokenade mistake list` yourself. Recorded dead ends are now recalled to the agent at the start of a session, most recent first, with the total count and a pointer to the full list. If you have recorded nothing, nothing is added and nothing is billed to you.
- `tokenade index` no longer leaves a stale copy of the index in your repository. It rewrote `.tokenade/index.json` on every run: 22.2 MB on this repository, four times the weight of the real index, inside your project tree, and stale by the next query. That file is now only a migration artefact, read solely while the index has not yet been taken over, and it is removed once the index is in place, as its semantic counterpart already did. It is rewritten only if storing fails, the one case where it becomes the only usable copy again.
- `tokenade index --force` really does rebuild what the other commands read. It announced `full (forced)`, and the next question still answered from the old content: the rebuild was written to a secondary file that is only read during a migration, while `query`, `impact`, `map` and `semantic` went on loading the previous index. This is exactly the command to run when tokenade itself has changed (a grammar, an extraction rule), the one case no changed-file detection can see. `tokenade watch` benefits too. The `--json` output now carries a `stored` field, and the text line names the real destination.
- Asking for several pages or several searches in one call finally counts for what it saves. `tokenade web <url1> <url2> …` and `tokenade search "<q1>" "<q2>" …` are the form tokenade recommends to you: they fetch the targets in parallel and charge you one round trip instead of N. But the dashboard recorded only ONE flat line for the whole batch, where the same targets asked for one at a time are each credited: on real records, a batch of three pages showed about 26 times less than the same three pages asked for separately, and three grouped searches a third. Each page and each query now carries its own line, under the same name and the same rule as the single call. What a page is worth no longer depends on how you asked for it.
- A file tokenade can no longer read no longer loses its symbols. A source file re-saved in a legacy encoding (an `é` in Latin-1 inside a comment) is no longer valid UTF-8: the index then erased everything it knew about it, and `tokenade query` answered `no symbol matching` about a function sitting on line 1 of a file that was right there. It now keeps the last known symbols, and replaces them as soon as the file becomes readable again. Along the way, `tokenade index --json` no longer under-counts files and symbols, and a plain `query` stops rewriting the whole index on every call in a repository containing such a file.
- The savings reported no longer claim anything for an agent tokenade does not recognise. The figure shown by `gain` and the dashboard depends on what your agent would have paid WITHOUT tokenade, and that is measured agent by agent. An agent missing from that table — a newcomer, or a detection that named nobody — was until now given the HIGHEST credit: the entire raw HTML page, on no evidence at all. Replayed on real records, that credits 1.78× more than an agent actually measured. It now receives a flat floor credit that does not grow with the size of the page: the less we know, the less we claim. Along the way, GitHub Copilot in VS Code is counted as Copilot rather than separately.
- `tokenade query --context` shows source code again from a subdirectory. It answered `(file unreadable)` for a file that was present in the current directory, so the context around the match, which is the whole reason to prefer `query` over a `grep`, stopped coming out.
- `tokenade query --context N --json` finally returns that context. The flag was accepted and echoed back in the response (`"context_lines": 2`) but ignored: the results carried no lines at all. Every match now carries its `context`, identical to the text mode, with secrets masked as everywhere else.
- `tokenade semantic` now returns paths that open from your directory too. `query` already did; `semantic` did not, so the same search from a subdirectory gave you a path relative to the project root, unusable as written, while `query` gave you a correct one. The two commands now speak from the same base.
- The paths you get back open from wherever you run the command. Now that the index covers the whole project, its paths are relative to the root, but you may well be in a subdirectory. `query` answered `crates/app/src/main.rs`, a path that does not exist from where you are, and so was unusable as written. Paths are now relative to your current directory (`../../crates/app/src/main.rs`), and `tokenade impact file.rs` accepts a file from the directory you are standing in. From the project root, nothing changes.
- `tokenade query`, `impact`, `map` and `semantic` finally cover the whole project, not just the directory you launched them from. The index was stored under the current directory, so from a subdirectory `query` built an index of that subtree alone and answered "no symbol matching" for a symbol declared elsewhere in the same repository: a confident negative about code that exists. tokenade now walks up to the project root (`.git`, `.hg`, `.svn`, `.jj`), so there is one index per repository, shared wherever you happen to be. The walk stops at your home directory: if that is itself under git, it does not become the root of all your projects. A directory outside any repository stays its own root, as before.
- A project you consult every day no longer ages like an abandoned one. tokenade deletes the index of a project left unused for 60 days, and evicts the oldest when its store exceeds its maximum size, both relying on the index's last-write time. Since that write is skipped when nothing has changed, the date stopped moving for a repository you query without modifying: it could be deleted while in daily use, and rebuilding a large index takes minutes. Consulting a project now marks it as used without rewriting the index, at most once an hour so the mark costs nothing.
- When tokenade cannot read the call graph, it says so instead of answering "nobody calls this". Silence on the `called by:` line and a `"callers": []` in `--json` are answers: they assert that nothing calls this symbol. If the graph is unreadable, the line now says the answer is unknown, the JSON returns `null` rather than an empty array, and `impact` reports that its list of dependents is incomplete for that reason. A graph that was read and is genuinely empty remains a real answer.
- Agent mode was ranking its results without the call graph. It orders the symbols it finds by centrality — how much code leans on them — and since the graph became load-on-demand it was not asking for it: every score was zero and the ranking fell back to its default order without saying so.
- The statistics no longer credit a saving against an agent tokenade has not measured. When an output exceeds what your agent can display, tokenade counts a share of the truncated text, since you would have had to fetch it back by hand. That assumes we know the agent truncates: for an agent missing from the measurement table, tokenade assumed it, and assuming always went the way that inflates the figure. It now counts nothing in that case. The seventeen measured agents — Claude Code, Codex, Copilot, Gemini CLI, Cline, Aider, Windsurf, OpenCode, OpenClaw, Hermes, Pi, Kilo Code, Droid, Grok, Qwen, T3 Code, Antigravity — keep exactly their figures.
- `tokenade semantic`, `map` and `skeleton` no longer load the call graph they do not need. It is stored separately now, and only `query` and `impact` read it. On a 3,980-file project, `semantic` goes from 269 to 147 ms and index preparation from 172 to 52 ms; `query` goes from 253 to 214 ms.
- A deleted file finally takes its calls with it. The index did remove the symbols and imports of a vanished file, but not the calls leaving it: they stayed in the graph indefinitely, and a rename counts as a deletion. Since `query` started showing `called by:`, it was visible: the list named callers in files that no longer exist. A modified file was already handled correctly.
- `tokenade query` finally answers "who calls this", which its help already promised. The command's help announces "where is this defined, and who calls it"; the second half was returned nowhere, neither as text nor in `--json`, even though the call graph was being built and stored. Every query now adds a compact line: `called by: handleRequest (server.go:135), TestReadsNode (server_test.go:76) +12 more`. The graph is indexed by name: when several declarations share a name, the line says so rather than attributing the list to one of them at random. `--json` carries `callers` and `callers_total`. The list no longer repeats a caller that calls ten times from the same place, and the count reported is the one from before the cap.
- `tokenade query`, `map` and `impact` were rewriting the entire index on every call. These commands change nothing, yet each re-serialised the whole index and wrote it back to disk before answering, even when not one file in the project had moved. On a 3,980-file project that meant 51 MB written per query, and 74 ms of the 243 ms spent preparing the index. The index is now rewritten only when a file has actually changed: the query goes from 319 to 244 ms and writes nothing. A file added, modified or deleted is still picked up immediately.
- The call graph knew five languages badly, and "who calls X" was answering from a fraction of the truth. PHP declared four call forms and produced none at all; Go, C# and Swift lost every method call; Java named the receiving variable instead of the method called, which is worse than a missing edge; and constructing a class (`new Widget()`) did not count as calling it in TypeScript, JavaScript, C++, Scala and PHP, although for a class that is often the only call form there is. Measured on real repositories: 104,075 to 272,657 edges on a 3,980-file Go project (62% of call sites were missing), and 16,668 to 59,670 on a TypeScript project. The index grows accordingly (24.7 to 51.0 MB on the Go project) and a search there goes from 157 to 319 ms: that is the price of the data that was missing.
- `tokenade query` finally finds fields in Python, Ruby, JavaScript, TypeScript and PHP. These five languages do not DECLARE their fields: an object attribute is the `self.x = …` / `@x = …` / `this.x = …` / `$this->x = …` of the constructor, and the extractor was only looking for a declaration. As a result none of their fields was indexed — measured on a real Python repository, 653 distinct attribute names across 176 files, all unfindable. Across the 60 most-used attributes of that repository, `query` answered with the field 0 times (and 30 times with a function whose name merely contained the string, which is worse than no answer); it now answers 60 times out of 60, each line carrying its class: `field AuthenticationError.num_retries — exceptions.py:146`. A field assigned on several paths comes out once.
- The dashboard showed a net loss where you had saved hundreds of millions of tokens. Injected context is re-billed on every turn it stays in the window, symmetrically to the credit that folded content earns. The credit stops after twelve turns; the charge did not, and went on re-billing the running total indefinitely. The ceiling was put in place on 3 August, but the lines already written stayed at the faulty rate. They are now removed from your local statistics: on the test machine, the "overhead re-billed" line of the 30-day window falls from 38.5 M to 12.5 M tokens and the net total goes from −11.4 M back to +14.6 M. No correct line is touched, and the ledgers are copied into `~/.tokenade/backup-recalc-<date>/` before anything is rewritten.
- A stalled web search no longer holds your turn. When tokenade searches in place of the native search, it waited on the engines without limit: measured here, one search took 18 seconds before concluding it was unusable, while the native search, which did answer in the end, had been available the whole time. The gate now waits 8 seconds at most (four times the time a successful search takes) and then hands back. A search that answers in time is served as before.
- Framework-frame stripping in a stack trace was not being applied. The rules that drop `java.base/` and `sun.reflect` frames, Python and Ruby standard-library frames, and the `adding:` lines of `zip -r` all targeted indented lines, and the comparison removed the indentation before comparing, so none of them could ever match. Twelve rules across seven compactors, inert since they were written; they now apply, and the number of lines removed is reported as it is everywhere else.
- A JSON response whose list is wrapped in an array was not being summarised. `{"Instances": [...]}` was folded and the outlying element brought forward, but `{"Reservations": [{"Instances": [...]}]}` — the shape `aws ec2 describe-instances` returns, and that of a great many APIs — fell back to "the first 3 elements, plus 118 more". Across 121 instances of which one was terminated, the only one you are looking for was among the 118. The summary now passes through the wrapping array, names the path the way you would index it (`Reservations[0].Instances`) and brings out the element that differs.
- The summary of a JSON array reported a wrong count of distinct values above 200. The counter stops at 200 so as not to hold a million values in memory, which is deliberate, but it printed `id=200` whether the array held 200 or 4,000. Across 400 elements all different, "400 elements, 200 distinct identifiers" reads as "each identifier appears twice". The line now writes `200+` once it has stopped counting, and the exact figure while it is still counting.
- Starting a session no longer makes the agent wait for disk housekeeping. Once a day, a project's first session ran the cache cleanup — eviction, size ceilings, compaction — and did it before handing back. Measured here: 3.3 seconds on that step, against 38 milliseconds for all the rest of startup. The housekeeping now goes into a separate process that outlives the session, and startup keeps a thousandth of it.
- `tokenade web <url1> <url2> …` was summarising none of the pages. A single URL came back behind a recoverable summary; two URLs came back whole. Measured on Wikipedia: 3,976 bytes for one page, 122,050 for two, 586,101 for six — about 146,000 tokens in a single response, on exactly the batched call the documentation recommends. Each page is now summarised as though it were alone, with its own recovery pointer, and a short page still arrives whole.
- `tokenade exec` could hand back a megabyte of raw output. When the script's output resists the compactors — a log where every line differs — all that remained was the one-megabyte ceiling, about 260,000 tokens, on the very command whose promise is that only its result comes back. Measured: 1,048,678 bytes returned where the same output through `tokenade wrap` returned 3,013. The output now goes to the recoverable summary as everywhere else, and a small result still arrives whole. `--no-compact` continues to deliver the bytes as they are.
- `tokenade read` on a large source file was handing back almost the whole file. Comment stripping gained enough to be kept, and by being kept it prevented the path that would have folded the file properly. Measured on a 405 KB file: 294 KB returned, against 1.2 KB by simply declining to strip the comments. A lightening step is now kept only if what it produces fits in a context; beyond that the file goes back through the normal path and you get a recoverable summary. Small files are unchanged.
- An API key no longer follows a redirect. HTTP libraries drop the `Authorization` header when a 3xx response points at another host, but not `x-api-key`, `api-key` or `x-goog-api-key`, which are the ones Anthropic, Azure OpenAI and Google use. A request carrying a credential now refuses the redirect and fails cleanly (a 3xx is not a legitimate answer from a JSON API). Downloads still follow: they carry no credential, and a CDN is entitled to move a file.
- The files tokenade writes outside the repository were readable by every other account on the machine. The fallback cache keeps the complete raw text of every tool result, and each entry was created `0644`: the `0700` directory was the only lock, and a directory mode survives neither a restore, nor an `rsync`, nor an archive unpacked with its own permissions. Measured on a real installation: 817 files out of 828. Entries are now created `0600`, and an existing installation is repaired once.
- `~/.tokenade/last-report.zip` too, and it is the most sensitive file of the lot. The support bundle contains your complete agent transcripts; it was written `0644` because the list of files to protect was written by hand and did not name it. That list is now enumerated from the directory, and it also covers `bypass_lost.jsonl`, which contains command lines, and the rotated log archives.
- `tokenade scrub-secret` was ignoring rotated logs. A rotation renames `gain.rollup.jsonl` to `gain.rollup.jsonl.bak-<date>`, and the sweep selected by extension: the file that had just received the lines leaving the live log was not cleaned. `debug.log.1` was in the same position.
- The in-file search cache kept the file's text as it was, secrets included. `tokenade file-search` splits the file into chunks and keeps them in `~/.tokenade/file_search.db`, outside the repository, so a key written in the code was copied there whole, and neither `scrub-secret` nor `scrub-cache` looked at that file. Redaction now happens on the way in, on the stored text as well as on what the search runs against, and both cleanup commands cover that cache for anything written earlier. The search still finds the passage: only the secret's value goes.
- `tokenade evict-stale` was giving back only three of its five caches to the disk. It did clear stale rows from all five, then compacted three files: the pages freed in the other two stayed in the file, with the text still readable inside. Measured here: a third of one cache was dead space containing source code deleted weeks earlier. All five are compacted now.
- A credential written in a comment no longer enters the semantic index. It entered because comments are what makes meaning-based search work, and cleaning it afterwards achieved nothing: the source is intact, so the next reindex put it straight back. Redaction now happens on the way in, and the other words of the comment stay searchable. `tokenade scrub-cache` also covers that index for caches written before this fix.
- `tokenade scrub-secret` reported "no occurrence" for a secret it was holding. A credential written in a source COMMENT reaches the semantic index (`~/.tokenade/semantic.db`), which is what makes meaning-based search work, and the emergency tool was not looking at that file. That is the worst possible answer, because you stop looking. The project indexes concerned are now deleted (they rebuild on the next query) and the file is compacted afterwards, since otherwise the string stayed readable in the freed pages. The sweep also covers every `.jsonl` log rather than two of them.
- After an update, `tokenade index` really does rebuild the index. It read the project cache without checking which version had written it, so it answered "incremental" and reused the previous version's symbols, while `query`, `impact` and `semantic` correctly refused that same file. The binary was giving two answers to one question.
- `tokenade index --force` rebuilds without reusing anything. Incremental mode asks the disk whether a file has changed, and the answer is no when what changed is tokenade itself. The only remedy was to delete two files by hand. The semantic index is rebuilt too, and `--json` now reports `semantic_mode` alongside `mode`.
- Accessibility snapshots (`tokenade snapshot`) no longer carry the nodes that announce nothing. An image with no alternative text, and a visual separator that arrived in the tree as text, say nothing to anyone and can be clicked by no one. That includes the ones carrying a reference which then turns out to lead nowhere: 55 remained on a single one of our reference pages, and none remains now. Anything carrying a name or a genuinely clickable reference is kept, without exception.
- `tokenade impact` finds the callers that name your module. It dropped every call whose name is declared in more than one file, and those are precisely the generic names (`fold`, `new`, `compact`), hence precisely the ones written qualified: `crate::logs::fold(…)` already said which one. On this repository one module goes from 1 reported dependent to 58 real ones, another from 26 to 68. An UNqualified call is still set aside, and if two files carry the same module name, nothing is guessed.
- `tokenade impact` names what it used to list twice. It returned `impl (6): TokenadeError ×6` — six different blocks folded into "six times the same thing". It now shows the six traits, and it qualifies a name only when that distinguishes something: two structs declaring `Timeout` give `Server.Timeout` and `Client.Timeout`, while a file with a single enumeration keeps its variants bare instead of repeating its name on each one.
- A method reads with the type that declares it, in every language. `tokenade query fmt` returned twelve bare `fn fmt`, and one file in this repository declares eight `fn agent`: nothing said which type. You now read `fn TokenadeError.fmt`, `fn Denial.fmt`. A single step covers Rust, Python, TypeScript, Java and Go. Symbols still sharing a label within one file drop from 321 to 225.
- A struct field reads with the type that declares it. `tokenade query Timeout` returned four `field Timeout` that only the path distinguished; you now read `field ServerConfig.Timeout`. This is the largest of the three ambiguities in this series: across the 517 indexed repositories on this machine, 38,126 of 90,188 fields (42%) share a bare name with a neighbour in their own file. On a real Go project, same-file duplicates fall from 615 to 9. Asking for a type name also brings back what it declares.
- A Go method now reads with the type it belongs to. On a real Go project, 42 methods are called `Name`, 41 `Description` and 34 `Execute`: `tokenade query Execute` returned 34 lines differing only by path. You now read `method (*MCPToolProxy) Execute`. Duplicates within one file go from 85 to 0, and asking for a type name brings back the type AND its methods, declaration first.
- `tokenade query` finally tells apart the `impl` blocks of one type, and knows who implements a trait. Six `impl` blocks on `TokenadeError` produced six identical lines: the trait, the only thing that distinguishes them, was indexed nowhere. You now read `impl Display for TokenadeError`, `impl From<std::io::Error> for TokenadeError`, and `tokenade query Display` lists the implementers, which it could not do at all. On this repository, `impl` lines indistinguishable from a neighbour drop from 76 to 4. The trait's declaration keeps first place ahead of its implementers.
- A `cargo test` whose filter matches nothing no longer passes for a green suite. Cargo answers `test result: ok. 0 passed; … 7171 filtered out` — the words a genuinely green suite ends with — and the tool let the line through. Across this machine's logs, 124 test runs out of 6,366 (1.9%) ran nothing and answered `ok`. The output now says `NOTHING RAN: the filter matched no test`, and only when NO target ran a test: a filter touching one crate in five is normal. Compiler warnings are kept.
- `tokenade semantic` finds the file that declares a symbol when you type its name. A compound name only reached the search split into words (`dropped_error_lines` becoming `dropped` + `error` + `lines`), never whole, so any file talking about errors and lines competed on equal terms with the one declaring the symbol. Measured across 151 real declarations, the right file comes first in 89% of cases against 64%, and the number of times it appeared nowhere at all falls from 12 to 1. Natural-language queries are unchanged. The semantic index grows by 17%.
- An update rebuilds the semantic index instead of answering from the old one. That index is the product of a tokeniser which changes from version to version, and nothing noticed: no file had changed, so the index passed for fresh. A search improvement reached nobody who had already indexed their repository.
- Outputs in columns of figures no longer under-estimate themselves. tokenade counted a corridor of spaces right-aligning a number as plain indentation, and so under-valued by 10 to 30% what `vmstat`, `df`, `netstat`, `ss` and `kubectl` really cost, which left the folding and fallback thresholds too loose for those outputs and the reported savings too low. Measured against the real tokenizer: `df` goes from −20% to −10% error, `ss` from −15% to −7%, `kubectl` from −10% to +1%. Indentation in front of code is counted as before.
- An OpenAPI spec named `openapi.json` finally reaches its compactor. The `.json` extension routed to the generic JSON compactor and hid the one written for OpenAPI specs, source maps and Jupyter notebooks, even though that is almost always the name you find them under. Measured on a real spec: 901 tokens returned as 106. An ordinary `.json` keeps exactly its previous behaviour.
- A `.log` file is no longer treated as a format in its own right. The extension routed to the generic log compactor even when the content was recognisable — a `git log`, a journald dump, an access log, cargo output, line-delimited JSON. Those files now go to their own compactor, and a `.log` tokenade cannot identify keeps exactly its previous behaviour. Measured across 1,277 real `.log` files: 11,070,257 tokens returned as 3,590,246, with no file returned larger.
- Security: the local model server refuses an externally reachable address. `TOKENADE_LOCAL_ADDR` was taken as given, so `0.0.0.0:8919` published an unauthenticated inference server on every interface. It now applies the same rule as the LLM proxy, with the same explicit escape hatch (`TOKENADE_LOCAL_ALLOW_REMOTE=1`) if that is what you wanted. The default, `127.0.0.1:8919`, does not change.
- Security: the LLM proxy accepts only an origin-form request target. A target not beginning with `/` was appended to the upstream host name, which let another process on the machine send the request — and your API key with it — to a different host.
- Three compactors finally say what they are throwing away. A connection table (`netstat`, `ss`) returned no address outside the listening ports, an `strace` returned no file path, and a line-delimited JSON stream showed its first and last lines without ever naming the middle, in all three cases without a line to say something was missing. Each now declares it, with the exact count, in the same terms as the other blocks of the same output.
- A single-project Gradle build names its tasks. With no project name to list, tokenade gave up folding and returned the whole log: 760 tokens instead of 110.
- Gradle build output finally names its projects. tokenade summarised it in a single line — `Gradle BUILD SUCCESSFUL: 33 tasks (0 failed)` — without one project or task name, while the same function already kept the modules of a Maven build. It now lists the projects, as Maven does: 877 tokens returned as 55 instead of 11, and the fold becomes usable.
- A summary that has become too small to still be about what it summarises (an `strace` returned as a bare count of system calls, every file path lost, for instance) is now refused on both of tokenade's paths rather than one. The raw content is returned instead of a summary that no longer answers the question asked.
- A command containing a pipe is compacted at last. Until now, as soon as a `|` appeared tokenade handed back the whole output untouched: `grep … | head -400`, `cat log | tail -200`, `kubectl get pods | grep web` cost every byte. tokenade now routes those outputs by their own shape, and returns what the same command without a pipe would return. Measured: 34,290 to 308 bytes on JSON log lines, 27,697 to 276 on 500 search results, 4,373 to 827 on a real `grep -rn`.
- Output requested down to the byte (`… | head -c 4096`) is still returned as it is, exactly as without a pipe, and a `--verbose` / `--debug` / `--nocapture` flag remains an explicit request for raw bytes that tokenade does not touch.
- A build that prints an error and then redraws its progress bar over it no longer loses the diagnostic: the error line AND the file:line beneath it survive the redraw, while the rest of the frame is still folded.
- Under GitHub Copilot CLI, tokenade now rewrites shell commands before they run, as it does under Claude Code. That is where most of the saving is: raw output never enters the context at all, instead of entering it and being folded afterwards. Copilot exposes this hook and tokenade was not using it, so Copilot users had only result folding.
- The message shown at install time for an agent other than Claude Code no longer claims that agent has no compatible hook. That is untrue for Copilot and Codex, which have one and which tokenade uses. It now says the option concerned targets Claude Code in particular, and names what the agent actually benefits from.
- tokenade recognises the session under all three names agents give it, rather than one. On an agent naming it differently, tokenade would believe every turn was new: it would re-inject its instructions on each message instead of once, and its read cache would miss. No agent supported today is in that position; the change closes the door before one is.
- A progress bar that redraws a BLOCK of lines by moving the cursor back up — the form BuildKit, recent npm and cargo's multiple bars use — is now replayed instead of being transcribed frame by frame. tokenade already folded single-line rewrites; these came through whole. Four lines redrawn thirty times arrived as a hundred and twenty lines and 4,677 bytes, and now arrive as five lines and 151 bytes: the ones the terminal actually displayed, with nothing to recover since nothing is lost. The same reading applies to the other way of refreshing a display, the screen clear that `watch` and `docker stats` use: twenty-five refreshes of `watch kubectl get pods` go from 7,738 to 309 bytes, with the last state complete. The replay happens before tokenade decides what the bytes are, which matters: the same file named `.log` came back as 4,301 bytes because its extension routed it to another compactor that saw only lines, and it now comes back as 173.
- Two lossless cleanups tokenade applied only to the commands it runs itself — shortening the project's absolute paths and folding long rules of dashes — now apply to what it reads as well: files, MCP tool results, script output.
- A line-delimited JSON stream now says how many lines were not JSON. The summary announced "12 JSON lines" on a thirteen-line document without mentioning the thirteenth, whether it was plain text or a truncated line, which is the common case on a log cut mid-write. A document where no line parses is still returned intact.
- A folded GraphQL schema now says what it left out. The fold keeps only type names, so each type's fields and every line that is not a declaration vanished without a word: 244 bytes came back as 67 bytes of counters. An elision line now names the number of lines removed. A schema where nothing is recognised is still returned intact rather than replaced by that line.
- Under Codex, a subagent now receives the same tokenade briefing as under the other agents: the subagent-start event was not registered, although Codex offers it and the briefing already existed elsewhere. Nothing to approve by hand, the installer handles it as for the other Codex hooks.
- The subagent briefing now answers in the shape the host agent expects, rather than a fixed one: Copilot reads it at the root of the response, Codex and Grok in their dedicated block, and Codex's schema rejects any key it does not know. The old response combined both shapes, the one form Codex refuses.
- A comma-separated data file is no longer taken for a list of paths when its columns contain URLs. A 300-line `.csv` came back as 191 bytes ("299 entries"), headers included; it now goes to the table compactor, which keeps the header and the values and declares what it truncates.
- A source file whose bibliography numbers its references `[1]`, `[2]`, `[3]` is no longer taken for a kernel log. Three citations of that form were enough: a 10,203-byte Python test module came back as 250 bytes of systemd summary, the rest discarded. tokenade now requires of the timestamp the fractional part the kernel always writes (`[ 1.234567]`) and a citation never has.
- After an automatic context compaction, tokenade reminds the agent which outputs it can recover without re-running anything. It offered the most recent ones, which in practice gave a list of fragments announced as "0k" while the same session had just folded several megabytes. It now offers the largest — the ones whose loss actually costs — and offers none too small to be worth the space it takes.
- A command killed by a signal now says so. A segfault returned no output at all, and a command killed part way through looked like a command that had finished saying nothing, with the compaction banner even suggesting the brevity came from tokenade. The message now names the signal (`SIGSEGV`, `SIGKILL`, often the out-of-memory killer) and makes clear that what precedes is what the command had time to write, not a result. `SIGPIPE` stays silent: `… | head -20` closes the pipe deliberately.
- When tokenade lightens a file by removing its comments, it now keeps the ones the toolchain reads: Go build tags (`//go:build`, `// +build`, `//go:generate`), Python declarations (`# type: ignore`, `# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-`, `# noqa`, `# pragma: no cover`), `# frozen_string_literal` in Ruby, TypeScript, ESLint, PHPStan, RuboCop and Checkstyle directives, `//# sourceMappingURL`, `SPDX-License-Identifier` — and, on the configuration side, `# syntax=docker/dockerfile:…` in a Dockerfile, `-- noqa` in SQL, `protolint`, `cmake-lint`. Those lines change what the file IS, not just what it says: the `syntax=` directive picks the engine that builds the image, and a file edited from a stripped version would have lost them.
- Security fix. A secret in a source file above about 3,000 bytes (outside a git repository) could be handed back in the clear by `tokenade read`, while the same secret in a smaller file, or in a text file, was masked: the size of the file decided. Redaction is now applied where the source content is prepared, and therefore on every surface that delivers it.
- A command where a variable set at the start is read again later (`S=/tmp/out; mytool > $S/f; cat $S/f`) was no longer compacted at all: splitting it would have lost the variable, so tokenade gave up. It is now wrapped as a single unit, which preserves the variable and restores compaction. Chains containing a `cd`, an `export` or a `source` whose effect must survive are still left alone.
- A file containing plain separator rules (`------------------`, as every font licence draws) is no longer taken for a sequence of commands split into sections. The documents concerned are served intact, and the exception traces that fell into the same trap now reach the trace compactor: 4,872 bytes become 1,974.
- A log whose lines carry the time without the date (`[14:25:48.462] [INFO] …`, the form most JS tooling uses) was not recognised as a log, and its diagnostic lines made it look like markdown. It now reaches the log compactor: a 29,018-byte storybook log returned as-is falls to 13,086, and an npm build failure from 68,306 to 12,364. No markdown document is affected.
- The very first question asked with `--prompt` on a machine downloads the retrieval model (~132 MB, once) and could stay silent for a minute and a half, which reads as a hang. It now says what it is doing before it waits.
- `tokenade read --prompt "…"` on a text file (`.md`, `.txt`, `.log`) wrongly announced that the question had not been applied and that what followed was not an answer. It was: on a 14,487-byte document, 678 bytes ending on the passage that answers, against 3,084 without the question. The misleading warning is gone; when no passage answers, the note saying so stays.
- A log whose lines begin with a bracketed timestamp (`[14:25:48.462] [INFO] …`) is no longer taken for a JSON array: it reaches the compactor that knows how to read it. An envoy access log goes from 3,582 bytes returned as-is to 521. No valid JSON is affected.
- Content that a detector claims without folding anything is no longer returned as-is: generic folding takes over, as it already did when tokenade ran the command itself. This mainly concerns what arrives with no filename — MCP tool results, standard input, unknown extensions. A document nobody claims is still served whole.
- A `cd DIR && …` command whose remainder spans several lines is no longer treated as one command: the part that followed was wrapped as a block, which prevented compaction from applying to each command separately.
- The `version` field of a JSON document no longer disappears. It was treated as a meaningless internal counter, so a `package.json`, an OpenAPI document or a tool's `--version --json` output came back without the version it was announcing. Genuine internal counters (`__v`, `resourceVersion`) are still removed.
- In an API schema, a parameter named `requestId`, `traceId` or `correlationId` is no longer deleted along with its description: those names are removed only when they actually carry an identifier, not when they document a parameter you might need to use.
- A JSON document where a single key carries the data — `{"users": […]}`, a schema under `definitions`, an object reduced to its `$ref` — could come back EMPTY, reduced to `{}`. Those names were treated as decoration when they are the data; they are now treated that way only in the kind of document where they really are.
- A large structured JSON file — an API schema, a configuration printed in the clear — could come back whole, uncompacted: the compact form tokenade produced reprinted the entire surround of the file with its original indentation, which made it more expensive than the file itself. It is now compact from end to end, without losing anything.
- A web search could block the agent for up to 23 seconds before handing back: when an engine did not answer within its deadline, tokenade started again with a deadline nearly twice as long. Retrying is meant for an immediate refusal, not for a slow engine; the maximum wait goes from 24.6 to 8 seconds per engine.
- A command written across several lines that starts with `cd` — the `cd` on its own line, the real work on the next — was not compacted at all, and its output came back to you whole. A newline now separates two commands exactly as a semicolon does, so the `cd` still applies to your following commands and the output of the rest is folded.
- A subagent sometimes received, for its very first command, a bare "output identical to the previous turn" marker and five trailing lines, for output that had never entered its context and that it had no way to retrieve. It shares its parent's session identifier, which was enough to attribute the parent's deliveries to it. A subagent is now served in full, while keeping the right to fold its own repetitions.
- On Gemini CLI, Qwen Code, Kilo and the agents of that family, a moved tokenade binary — an npm reinstall, an automatic update — would have caused every affected tool call to be REFUSED, the host reading the shell's error as a refusal decision. Hooks in that family now stay silent when they cannot run, as those for Claude Code already do.
- When the host agent truncates a command's output at its own ceiling, it reports that nowhere: the result stops mid-word, and nothing distinguishes a cut tail from a command that ends there. tokenade now says so, naming the ceiling and the number of missing characters, so that you do not reason about an incomplete result believing it whole.
- A heavily commented TOML file — a hand-written manifest, a documented `pyproject.toml` — was not recognised as TOML: its own comments counted against it. It came back to you whole, 21,711 bytes where the TOML compactor returns 3,849. A long licence header no longer hides the rest of the file either.
- An ordinary web page could come back to you as a list of search results: all it took was for the site to carry its own search box in the header. The article's text disappeared in favour of a numbered list of navigation links, a 96,692-byte page returned as 525. A page is now read as a results page only if it carries markup specific to a search engine.
- A configuration file with a comment mentioning a path — "stored in /work/" — could be read as a JavaScript stack trace and come back with almost everything cut: on a `docker-compose.yml`, 28,709 bytes returned as 854, with only the header comments left. A frame must now open its line, as it always does in a real trace.
- A Python, Rust or TypeScript file could be read as a `CMakeLists.txt` — all it took was a word like `project` opening a line, even inside a list of words within a string — and the CMake compactor then stripped its `#` comments. A CMake command must now be a call, which it always is in that language; real CMake files are recognised as before.
- A Rust, Python or TypeScript file could be read as a protobuf schema: `enum` was treated as a protobuf-specific keyword although it belongs to five other languages, and a named argument written `message=…` on its own line passed for a declaration. The protobuf compactor then removed the file's comments. Real `.proto` files are recognised as before.
- The Gemini hook modified a process environment variable (`TOKENADE_SESSION`) for the duration of a call to pass the session identifier, and restored it afterwards. In an active session, anything reading that variable at the same moment saw the wrong value. The identifier is now passed directly, and the product no longer writes that variable at all.
- A Python or TypeScript file containing a few SQL statements — a test schema, an example query — was treated as an entire SQL file, and the SQL reader then cut the comments from the surrounding code. SQL must now make up a real share of the file; a normal `.sql` file is recognised as before.
- A Python or TypeScript file could be read as SQL because of its `with …` lines (a CTE in SQL, a block in Python), and the SQL reader then cut every line containing `--`: a docstring mentioning `git log --name-only` lost everything after the dash. Real `.sql` files are still recognised.
- A source file whose header is a block of `//` comments came back to you presented as the contents of an archive — a `plain-listing — 1412 entries` banner, with the licence header and module documentation discarded. The `/` that made it look like a path was the comment marker itself.
- A source file quoting an Ansible `PLAY RECAP` banner came back to you replaced by `0 play(s), 0 task(s), 0 host(s), 0 failed, 0 changed` — the report of a run that never happened, in place of the file. A summary is no longer produced when there was nothing to read.
- A source file containing a snippet of `cargo bench` output came back as the report of that snippet, with the rest of the file discarded. The summary is now refused when the capture is only an island inside a larger file, and real `cargo bench` output is summarised as before.
- A shell script could be taken for `env` output and sent to the secret redactor: a script could come back with `<redacted>` in place of a command substitution, or with a line cut in the middle. A file starting with `#!` is never treated as an environment dump again; real dumps and `.env` files are redacted as before.
- A TypeScript, JavaScript or MDX file opening on imports written across several lines (`import {`, then the list, then `} from …`) was taken for Terraform. The Terraform compactor removes lines starting with `#`, and so every heading of a document. Real Terraform files are still recognised.
- `tokenade read <source file>` handed you the whole file while the same file read through the Read hook came back without its comments. The same rule applies on both sides now: a Go file loses about 20% of its bytes there, C 15%, Python and Rust 10%. A file you are editing (source in a git repository, under the ceiling) still arrives raw.
- A tiny or empty HTML page was given an explanatory note longer than itself. The note is now sent only if it costs less than the document, except on a page carrying a `<script>` — there the tag proves it is a shell, and the note stays useful whatever the size.
- The dashboard massively over-stated the input of Codex sessions: Codex counts its cache reads INSIDE `input_tokens` where Anthropic counts them separately, and we stored the raw figure. On a heavily cached session, the reported input could exceed the real input by a factor of thirty. Codex appeared far hungrier than Claude Code in the same column.
- An HTML page without JavaScript whose extraction returned nothing arrived EMPTY, without a word of explanation. It now carries the `[tokenade: thin-result]` note, with the causes that really match a script-free page (malformed markup, text inside custom elements) instead of the "client-side rendered" advice, which cannot apply.
- A lone `schema { query: Query }` block, perfectly valid GraphQL, came back empty: 47 bytes returned as 0. The anti-empty guard now delivers it intact.
- A `cargo` build log read from a file (`tokenade read build.txt`, a log opened by the agent) came back to you whole, while the same output passed through tokenade as the command ran was summarised. The summary applies on both sides now: on a real cold build of 420 crates, 13,493 bytes returned as 595, with the error and warning blocks kept complete. A document that quotes cargo output is not affected.
- A `.mts` TypeScript module came back to you as a video: a `[tokenade:video …]` banner, a whisper transcription started locally, and the entire source lost. `.mts` is indeed the extension of an AVCHD container, and it is also that of TypeScript ESM modules; the file's header now decides, so a real AVCHD clip is still recognised as video.
- `.pyx` (Cython), `.pyw`, `.hh` (C++ header) and `.fish` files were recognised as source code, and so never compacted, but no language was associated with them, so their comments were never removed either. They now follow the rules of Python, C++ and shell, which are exactly theirs.
- If loading the model failed once — an interrupted download, a full disk — the failure was remembered for the life of the process: semantic search and `tokenade read --prompt` stayed dead until a restart, retrying nothing. Only a successful load is remembered now, and two simultaneous first calls no longer download the model twice.
- A command of the form `cd <directory>` followed by a variable and then its use (`cd /project; W=…; npx tsc $W`), including written across several lines, was not compacted at all, and its output came back to you whole. The `cd` now stays in your shell and the rest is compacted as one block. Measured across 2,500 real commands: 61 commands uncompacted for this reason, 30 afterwards.
- The output of commands tokenade declines to rewrite out of caution — `ssh`, `vim`, an interactive session — came back to you whole, even though the fold was already computed behind the scenes and then discarded. It is served to you now, with its `expand-ref` pointer for recovering the original bytes. On a real `ssh -T machine 'ps -eo pid,tty,stat,time,cmd'`: 31,779 bytes returned as 294. Nothing changes on versions of Claude Code that cannot replace a result, nor on a command you deliberately ran through `tokenade raw`.
- A command preceded by a lowercase variable assignment (`f=/tmp/x cat $f`, `p=/tmp vim notes.txt`) had its program read from the assignment's VALUE — `x` instead of `cat`. Two consequences: the real program's compactor was never chosen, and above all an interactive program was no longer recognised as one, so `p=/tmp vim notes.txt` went into a wrapper with no terminal. Uppercase assignments already worked; both cases are handled alike now.
- The "this command would benefit from a dedicated compactor" advice could name `cd`, which produces no output to compact. It arrived on commands of the form `cd <directory> && <heredoc script>`, which tokenade cannot split: the whole command came back up and its first word was the `cd`. A word that acts on the shell is no longer proposed.
- A file that MENTIONS a package manager in prose — a `.gitignore` explaining what a `pnpm install` leaves behind — could come back to you replaced by `pnpm: 0 added, 0 warnings, 0 errors`: the report of an install that never happened, in place of the file. Measured on a real case: 6,865 bytes returned as 36. A summary that counts several things and finds none of them is no longer served, whichever compactor produced it.
- On GitHub Copilot CLI, tokenade was not installing its compaction hook: at the moment the conversation is summarised, the `expand-ref` pointers to already-folded bytes were neither protected nor recorded, and the recovery manifest was not written. This is what Claude Code users have had for a long time; Copilot has it now.
- The same on Grok: neither the compaction hook nor the one that gives a subagent its briefing. Both exist in the event table Grok documents itself; they are installed now.
- In a `cargo` build summary, a line that is neither a `Compiling`, nor the final line, nor an error or warning block was removed without being reported. The summary now counts those lines and says so. Output it does not recognise at all still comes back to you as it is.
- A source file QUOTING `kubectl describe` output (an example in a JSDoc comment, a fixture inside a string) was taken for kubectl output and came back to you empty. Four real files, two of them from tokenade's own code.
- A Rust file with a line containing `schema {` (an identifier such as `minify_schema`, or an error message) was taken for a GraphQL schema, and the file came back to you EMPTY, with no banner and no way to recover it.
- A compactor that returns nothing at all on a non-empty input now delivers the original text rather than the void. Eight real files were in that position, including three perfectly well detected HTML pages that an unclosed `<title>` made disappear entirely.
- A `.graphql` or `.gql` file is now recognised by its extension. Before, a schema whose header began with comments was not recognised at all and was sent to you whole.
- Source code that quotes build output is no longer mistaken for build output. A Rust file whose job is to parse Maven logs — so its own source is full of `[INFO]` strings, and its header comment says "Apache Maven" — came back as a two-page fake build summary with every function, struct and impl deleted. That compactor rebuilds from what it parses, so a wrong guess replaces your file rather than shortening it. Markers now only count when they are neither quoted nor in a comment. Checked against 10219 log files: every one still detected, including CI logs that prefix a timestamp.
- A file of English questions is no longer mistaken for SQL. `Explain` is a SQL keyword as well as the first word of half the questions anyone asks, and two such lines were enough: one real file of writing prompts came back as its comment header and nothing else, with all nineteen prompts gone. No SQL statement ends in a full stop or a question mark, so lines that do no longer count. Checked against 342 real .sql files — every one of them still detected — and 92 documents stopped being read as SQL.
- A document that quotes a config file no longer gets read as one. A 96 KB markdown plan that showed a `Cargo.toml` in a code block was treated as an INI file because of the `[package]` and `[dependencies]` lines inside that block; tokenade then stripped what it took for comments and all fourteen of the document's headings went with them. Code blocks are quotations, and the detectors that can be fooled by them now say so. A real config file that happens to contain a code fence still reads as a config.
- Documentation pages keep their headings. A markdown or MDX file that imports components — `import { Tabs } from "..."` — was being read as a Terraform config, because `import` is a Terraform keyword too. tokenade then stripped what it thought were comments, and a markdown heading starts with the same `#`: on one real docs page all six headings vanished, on another all four. Measured across 26683 markdown files, 265 were affected; one is now. One Terraform file in twenty-one loses its dedicated compactor and falls back to the general one, which costs a little compression and no content.
- A new session is no longer told it has been running the same command over and over. When tokenade sees a command produce byte-identical output several times in a row it says so and shortens the answer, which is right — but the counter was shared by every session on the machine, so a session running a command for the first time could be told it had already run it three times, and be handed the shortened answer with the output tail removed. The one that had never seen the output was the one refused it. The count is now per session.
- MCP tools keep their parameter descriptions again. tokenade was stripping them out of every tool list by default, which made manifests less than half the size but also deleted, for about one parameter in five, the only place that says what values are allowed or what unit to use — `Minimum severity to block (high, medium, low)` with no enum anywhere, `use {pattern_name} placeholder`, `in UTC`. Your agent had to guess. That was measured across 2415 parameters from 92 real tool manifests. The stripping is still available if you want the savings and can live with the guessing: set TOKENADE_MCP_LEAN_SCHEMA=1. Titles, examples and comments are still removed either way, since they never carry a rule.
- When `tokenade mcp-wrap` cannot use a config, it now tells you why in words you can act on. VS Code's mcp.json allows comments and trailing commas and your editor never complains about them, but they are not valid JSON — so tokenade refused the file with `expected value at line 2 column 3`, which reads like tokenade being broken. It still refuses (rewriting the file would delete every comment you wrote), and now it says exactly that and what to do instead. A `//` inside a URL is not mistaken for a comment.
- `tokenade mcp-wrap` now works on Codex and Grok configs written by PowerShell. Those two keep their MCP servers in TOML, and a file carrying a UTF-8 BOM — which PowerShell adds by default — was parsed correctly and then silently skipped: nothing wrapped, nothing written, no error, so every MCP result kept arriving at full size while the command reported success. Unwrapping had the same blind spot and would have left half the entries behind. Your file keeps its BOM; tokenade no longer trips over it.
- The credential scrub for documents now runs inside the extractor itself, so no future route can skip it. Every place tokenade turns a document into text was audited: five of them, and the four that were already scrubbing each did it in a different way, which is exactly how the fifth — the web-fetched document fixed just above — ended up with no scrub at all. Nothing else was leaking; this closes the shape of the bug rather than another instance of it.
- A PDF, DOCX or spreadsheet fetched from a URL is now scrubbed for credentials before you see it, the way a local one already was. Reading such a file from disk removed keys and tokens from the extracted text; fetching the same file over the web did not, on either of the two routes that show it to you — the text displayed inline, and the passages returned when you ask a question about the document. That second one hands back raw matching lines, so asking about anything near a key printed it. The copy saved to disk was already being scrubbed. A URL is the case you chose least, so it should have been the most careful.
- A `cd` on its own line no longer swallows the command after it. Writing `cd some/dir` then `ls -la` on the next line routed everything through the `cd` filter, so the real command's compactor never ran — the same two commands joined with `;` or `&&` worked fine. That is the shape agents type most. `ls -la` in a 60-file directory now comes back at 840 bytes with its columns elided instead of 1388 with a plain line count. Multi-line loops, heredocs and `$(...)` are still left alone, as they must be.
- The LLM proxy no longer declines a fold that costs nothing. Its cache gate weighs what a rewrite saves against the prompt cache it invalidates, but it counted the whole request after the fold point as cached — including everything the provider was never caching. If your client caches only the tools and system prompt, as Anthropic's own guidance suggests, the entire message history is uncached and folding a tool result inside it invalidates nothing; the gate was charging 50000 tokens of cache write for it and refusing, on a 300 KB conversation. Clients that also cache their last messages, like Claude Code, still get the fold declined there, because that prefix really does run to the end.
- `head -c N` and `tail -c N` now return exactly the bytes you asked for. On a source file large enough to look like a browse, tokenade was stripping the comments out of a byte-precise read: `head -c 6000` on a Rust file gave back 4976 bytes, matching the file for the first 3251 and then quietly diverging, with no banner on the output and nothing on stderr. Checking a header, comparing a prefix or computing an offset got a wrong answer that looked right. Line-based reads (`head -400 file.rs`) still fold as before. Measured across 80286 recorded commands, a byte count asks for 300 bytes at the median, so nothing worth folding is lost.
- A patch is now read as a patch whatever language it modifies. `git diff` piped in was routed by the language of the files it CHANGES, so a diff of markdown files was folded as a document and a diff of JavaScript as SQL — and the SQL route deleted the header of every hunk, because it treats `--` as a comment and `--- a/dist/dev.cjs` starts with two of them. You got the changed lines with no way to tell which file they came from, and on a rename neither name. 30 of the 256 patches measured were affected; 0 now. A prose document that merely SHOWS a diff in a code block is untouched — five of those were checked.
- tokenade's local LLM proxy now installs on a machine that runs its own model. It refused to, on every one of them: the check that stops the proxy pointing at itself judged on the HOST alone, so anything served from localhost — Ollama, LM Studio, vLLM, llama.cpp — looked like tokenade talking to tokenade and the install was declined. It now judges on the port and, when that is ambiguous, asks the thing on the other end whether it is us. The proxy also installs itself and comes back on its own if it stops answering, and survives a kill or a reboot on Linux, macOS and Windows.
- The savings ticker now runs on six agents instead of two. Copilot CLI and Cursor CLI show it on their status line, Pi in its footer, and aider — which has no status line of any kind — gets it once per launch through the only surface it exposes. Eleven other agents were measured and genuinely have nowhere to put it.
- Antigravity's command-line agent is now driven by a hook like every other agent, instead of being reachable only through the MCP proxy. Its commands are routed through tokenade before they run, and it asks you first rather than approving them for you.
- Fixed: an agent configuration file that exists but cannot be read — tightened permissions, non-UTF-8 bytes, a directory in the way — was read as if it were empty and then written back, leaving it holding tokenade's settings and nothing of yours. Six places did this, including the one that appends to your global CLAUDE.md and then reports "appended". A file we cannot read is now left alone and named.
- Fixed: a file whose name ends in a number lost the dot before its extension when a long listing was folded — `f300.txt` came back as `f300txt`. An agent rebuilding the name from that concludes the file is not there, which is the one mistake a fold must never cause.
- Fixed: `tokenade upgrade` could not succeed on a connection slower than about a megabyte a second, whatever its health. The deadline documented as "per read" was in fact a deadline on the whole download, so a 130 MB binary had to arrive inside two minutes or fail. It now only gives up when nothing is arriving at all.
- Fixed: `git log` was folded about sixteen times less than it should have been. Every entry begins with the word `commit`, which is also a SQL keyword, so two commits were enough for the output to be treated as SQL source and passed through almost whole. 51 KB came back as 50 KB; it is now 3 KB, with every commit id kept.
- Fixed: a `Cargo.lock` was folded about thirteen times less than it should have been, for the same reason in a different place — a lockfile is valid TOML, so the generic branch claimed it before the one written for lockfiles. 135 KB came back as 135 KB; it is now 10 KB, with every package name kept.
- Fixed: `<hash>`, `<pattern>`, `<file>` and every other placeholder of that shape were deleted from documentation, including inside code spans. `tokenade expand-ref <hash>` reached the agent as `tokenade expand-ref ` — a command with its argument silently removed, and nothing saying the text had been altered. Anything that is not a known HTML element is now text, and nothing inside backticks is touched at all.
- Fixed: `expand-ref --raw` handed back everything at once, with no ceiling. On a large fold that was seven megabytes in a single answer, from the command whose whole purpose is to make recovery cheap. It now returns a generous first slice and the exact command to continue from where it stopped; `--budget 0` still gives you the lot.
- Fixed: when the directory tokenade indexes could not be read — a detached worktree, an unmounted share, a directory swapped mid-scan — every file it knew about was treated as deleted and the index was replaced with an empty one. `query` and `map` then answered "not found" about code that exists. It now refuses to replace an index with nothing and says why.
- Fixed: an MCP configuration written by PowerShell was invisible to tokenade. Those files start with three bytes that PowerShell adds by default, and three separate places rejected the file because of them — one of them silently, so the configuration simply appeared not to exist.
- Fixed: the savings figure for long sessions was overstated. The counter that tracks how much content a session has been handed was being read from a filename that nothing writes, and parsed in a way that could not have worked anyway, so the point at which a conversation would have been compacted was never reached and credit kept accruing past it.
- The dashboard's "where the savings come from" no longer credits `cd`. A command that begins by changing directory had the whole result attributed to the navigation rather than to the work, whenever the separator was a semicolon or a line break.
- Fixed, on Windows: ten places where tokenade starts a helper process flashed a console window on your desktop, including the status line, which runs several times a second.
- Package and service metadata dumps — `pacman -Qi`, `apt show`, `dpkg -s` — are now folded when they arrive as a file or a paste, not only when tokenade runs the command itself. 33 KB came back whole; it is now under 3 KB, with the record count stated and the rest one command away. Nearly a third of those bytes are the same field names repeated for every package.
- Fixed: `journalctl` was not folded at all on any machine whose language is not English. journald prints the month name in your locale, and the parser could read exactly one of them — it required an uppercase first letter and then counted bytes, so `aout 25 …` failed twice over, and output came back whole. It is now read by shape rather than by position, which also picks up the English form with a space-padded day.
- Fixed: `lsof` came back untouched. Listing every open file prints the same header as listing network connections, so it was handed to the compactor that knows about connections and which has nothing to say about files: 113 KB in, 113 KB out. It is now folded to about two fifths, and `lsof -i` still gets the connection compactor that cuts it to a tenth.
- Fixed: `ps` and `lsof` output pasted into the conversation, or read from a file, is now folded exactly as hard as when tokenade runs the command itself. It was falling back to the generic table fold instead of the compactor written for it — about seven times weaker for `ps` — so the same bytes cost seven times more depending only on who ran the command.
- Command output in a non-UTF-8 encoding is now decoded instead of destroyed.
